"knowledge/hardware/gpu-kernel-pipelines.md", "sha256": "4db609a35cae2c7bd05603e026fc76d3e1281111ca2dad54d1a319851b3b498a", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:4500]\n# K-HW-011：GPU 矩阵与归约流水：布局、资源和完成依赖\n\n一个 GPU kernel 的设计同时决定数据怎样进入计算单元、哪个线程持有什么片段、临时状态占多少资源，以及何时允许覆盖。tile 更大、流水更深或矩阵指令更多，都只有在这些关系成立时才可能改善性能。\n\n## GEMM 的数据依赖与资源账\n\n对 C=A·B，令输出块为 Mₜ×Nₜ、沿归约轴每次处理 Kₜ。一个阶段读取 A 的 Mₜ×Kₜ 片段和 B 的 Kₜ×Nₜ 片段，输出 accumulator 跨多个 K 阶段保留。数据依赖是：全局读取 → 片上数据就绪 → 矩阵乘加 → accumulator 可消费 → epilogue → 输出可见。下一个 K 阶段的读取可以提前，但不能覆盖仍被矩阵指令读取的片上缓冲。\n\n若输入每元素 s 字节、流水深度 n，单 block 的输入缓冲主项为 n·s·(MₜKₜ+KₜNₜ)，输出累加主项为 a·MₜNₜ，a 为累加元素字节。还要计入对齐、barrier、转置区、地址与 epilogue 临时量。共享存储、寄存器、线程数和 block 数约束共同限制驻留；仅检查共享存储够用不足以证明没有 spill。\n\n设定 Mₜ=Nₜ=128、Kₜ=64、输入 2 字节、累加 4 字节。每级输入缓冲为 32 KiB，三级为 96 KiB；输出 accumulator 为 64 KiB。若由 128 个线程均摊，仅输出就平均需要每线程 128 个 32 bit 寄存器，尚未计其他变量。改成两级减少 32 KiB 片上缓冲，但能否增加驻留 block，还取决于寄存器与目标架构限制。这个算例描述资源占用，不指定任意 GPU 的最佳 tile。\n\n## 先适配指令布局，再安排线程\n\n矩阵指令要求的 fragment 布局不等于用户矩阵的行列布局。全局合并访问、共享存储 bank、矩阵操作数布局与输出写回应作为一条路径比较。局部转置或 swizzle 增加地址/搬运工作，却可能减少非合并事务和 bank 冲突；也可能让后续消费者再支付一次转换。\n\n固定来源中，[FlashAttention-3 v1](../sources/S-FLASHATTENTION3.md) §2–3 以 Hopper 的 TMA、异步 WGMMA 和 warpgroup 组织流水，warpgroup 由 4 个 warp 组成。这是具体架构上的实现入口。[ROCm 固定文档](../sources/S-ROCM.md) 则描述 CDNA 的 MFMA、VGPR/AGPR 与 LDS。两条路径的线程片段、寄存器资源和异步能力需要分别核实，不能将 CUDA 指令、32 线程约定或某一 bank 数直接替换成 AMD 名称。\n\n相同 dtype 也可能有不同操作数布局限制。FA3 对 FP8 的讨论说明，前一 GEMM 的 accumulator 要交给后一 GEMM 时，转换与排列是实际成本；FP16 路径可用不意味着只更改 dtype 就能得到合法 FP8 kernel。低精度还要重新验证 scale、累加、异常值和任务质量。\n\n## 一个完整的非矩阵指令映射：两 warp 共享输入\n\n先把逻辑算法落实到可检查的普通线程运算，再考虑改用矩阵指令。定义 A∈ℝᴹˣᴷ、B∈ℝᴷˣᴺ，均按行连续保存，输出 C∈ℝᴹˣᴺ；本例只计算 C=AB，不读取旧 C，没有 bias 或其他 epilogue。输入和输出为 FP32，累加也为 FP32；数学参考使用实数求和，真实运算是否融合乘加及其舍入另行核验。维度可为零，K=0 时有效输出为零，M=0 或 N=0 时不启动输出工作。\n\n选择一个 CTA 的输出 tile 为16×32，归约阶段为8，CTA有64线程。输出块坐标为 (m₀,n₀)=(16bₘ,32bₙ)，阶段 j 的归约起点 k₀=8j，共 J=⌈K/8⌉ 阶段。这个小例子旨在展示映射与生命周期，没有声称它能接近矩阵单元吞吐。\n\n线程编号 τ=32w+λ，其中 w∈{0,1} 是 warp，λ∈[0,31] 是 lane。warp w 独占本输出块第16w至16w+15列。lane λ 保存8个累加量，令 u∈[0,7]，i=λ+32u，则它负责局部坐标 r=⌊i/16⌋=2u+⌊λ/16⌋、c=16w+(i mod16)。64×8=512个坐标恰好覆盖16×32输出，没有重复写者，也不需要跨 warp 合并输出。\n\n每个输入槽保存行主序共享矩阵 Aˢ[16,8] 和 Bˢ[8,32]。全体64线程按线性下标搬运：Aˢ 的 a=τ+64v，v=0,1，映射为 (⌊a/8⌋,a mod8)；Bˢ 的 b=τ+64v，v=0,1,2,3，映射为 (⌊b/32⌋,b mod32)。每个共享位置有唯一写者。若全局基址是 pA、pB、pC，字节地址为：\n\n- A：pA+4[(m₀+r)K+(8j+k)]，前提 m₀+r<M 且8j+k<K。\n- B：pB+4[(8j+k)N+(n₀+c)]，前提8j+k<K 且 n₀+c<N。\n- C：pC+4[(m₀+r)N+(n₀+c)]，前提 m₀+r<M 且 n₀+c<N。\n\n非法输入位置填零，且不计算、解引用越界地址；非法输出位置不写回，但其线程仍参加规定的集体同步。对 K 尾块，A/B 的同一无效归约项都为零，因此补齐到8不改变有效 C。全局地址按各自实际 stride 计算，共享地址按本节定义的紧密布局计算，不能把两者混用。\n\n设 M=19、N=35、K=17，选最后输出块 m₀=16、n₀=32。j=1、局部 r=2、k=3、c=2 对应 A[18,11]、B[11,34]、C[18,34]，相对基址字节偏移分别为1268、1676、2656。j=2只有归约位置16有效；这个输出块只有3×3个合法输出。此处行跨度68/140字节，不具有统一的16字节行对齐；4字节元素复制合法，不能给整行虚构更强的对齐证明。\n\n消费者按 k=0…7 读 Aˢ[r,k] 与 Bˢ[k,c]，更新自己持有的8个累加量。两个 warp 共享 A，分别读取 B 的不同16列。以固定 u、k 的标量共享读取为例，同一 warp 的两组16 lane各读同一 A 值；B读取16个不同字，每字被两 lane读取。依据[共享存储广播与 bank 规则](../sources/S-CUDA-BEST.md)，相同字的多 lane读取是广播，不能按32次独立事务计费；若改变行跨度或lane坐标，需要重算实际冲突。协作写共享时，字下标 τ+64v 的 bank 为 λ，单条标量写指令覆盖32个不同 bank。\n\n这条路径已连续给出逻辑 tile、元素地址、线程归属、共享布局和输出写回。把标量 k 循环换成矩阵指令时，仍要另外证明形状、dtype、布局、参与线程和完成依赖；并非保持这份 lane 所有权就能直接替换指令。[Attention 的 warp 矩阵实例](../frameworks/attention-backends.md)采用公开的矩阵装载/存储接口交接，明确不假定内部 fragment 的 lane/寄存器排列。跨指令或平台应重新核对契约。\n\n## 从一个槽到多槽：每代数据的完整协议\n\n定义 d≥1 个槽，每槽的身份为 (s,g,j)，s=j mod d、g=⌊j/d⌋。一个槽从“空闲”到“复制中”，再到“可读”，最后到“读者全部结束”。槽内的 A/B 有同一个逻辑阶段 j，但可以由多条复制组成；仅完成 A 或某一线程发出的复制，不足以宣布整个槽可读。两个 warp 都是 A 的读者，各自又是对应 B 半块的读者；本例保守地将整个 A/B 槽一起释放。\n\n这里区分三类完成：复制完成使相应字节到达共享存储；具有正确作用域的同步使消费者能安全观察这些写入；每个消费者完成最后一次共享读取，才允许下一代覆盖。消费者若改成异步矩阵指令，线程发射返回也不等于指令结束读取；必须以该指令规定的完成条件释放。不同槽的复制可以提前，某槽的代次不能提前。\n\n下面是本例的一种完整组织。复制过程可由硬件异步操作承担，也可由同步加载实现；伪代码中的“等待”明确表达依赖，不指定某个 CUDA API 的机械一一替换。若把两个 warp 的完成合并为 CTA barrier，所有64线程都必须以一致顺序到达。\n\n```pseudocode\n输入：A、B、M/N/K、输出块坐标、d≥1\n所有lane的8个累加量初始化为0；建立d个空闲槽及各代次\n定义提交阶段(j)：\n    s←j mod d，g←floor(j/d)\n    确认槽s上一代全部复制/写者及两个warp读者已结束\n    设置本代身份(s,g,j)，清除本代完成状态\n    全体线程按上述地址划分：合法元素复制，无效元素写零\n    将本代所有输入写入纳入同一个完成契约；提交复制票据\n若J=0：只写有效输出零，结束\n启动：依次提交 j=0…min(d,J)−1\n对j=0…J−1：\n    等待身份(s,g,j)的全部复制与零填完成，并建立CTA可见性\n    每个warp按k=0…7消费A/B，更新它独占的输出累加量\n    每个warp在最后共享读取完成后到达本代“读完”条件\n    等待两个warp都读完；此后槽s才可释放\n    若j+d<J：提交阶段(j+d)，把新复制与后续已就绪阶段的计算重叠\n排空：确认全部已提交票据和消费者结束，不产生未消费阶段\n每个lane只向有效(m₀+r,n₀+c)写自己的8个结果\n```\n\nJ=1时只提交和消费阶段0；J=2时只消费0、1，不访问并不存在的“下一块”；J=0时不等待未创建的票据。d大于J时多出的槽为空闲，静态分配仍可能为它们付容量。每个阶段被消费恰好一次，输出在处理前 j 个阶段后等于这 j 段乘积之和；同一输出的累加次序始终沿 j、k递增，warp的不同调度顺序不改变其拥有的输出集合。\n\n取消也不能跳过已参与的集体同步，或让一部分线程提前退出。可以在统一边界停止提交新阶段，排空已提交复制和消费者，再放弃输出；若必须保留部分结果，则另外定义它对应哪些归约区间。本章的 C=AB 没有将部分 C 发布为完整结果的语义。\n\n本例采用“整个输入槽等两个warp”的保守释放。若仅等第一个warp、或只等复制票据结束，就可能在第二warp仍读取旧 A 时写入新阶段。若精细拆开A/B资源，A必须等两个warp，B各半块可以分别等对应warp；那需要独立身份和复用条件。减少等待的收益要与增加的同步状态比较，不是多计几个barrier就自动更可靠。\n\n## 抽象票据怎样落实到 CUDA 的同步契约\n\n固定 [CUDA 12.8 指南](../sources/S-CUDA-ASYNC\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:12000]\n际生成的指令路径、共享存储与寄存器、spill、内存事务及时间线，而不把源代码中的异步调用当成已经重叠的证据。正确性覆盖非整除尾部、全 mask 行、不同历史长度、GQA 映射和归约次序。\n\n短输入或小矩阵可能由启动与调度支配，深流水的启动/收尾反而较贵；长 prefill 更容易摊薄这些成本；长历史小 batch decode 可能受 KV 带宽与并行度约束。最终对照还要回到服务 SLO，避免局部 kernel 的工作区或驻留资源挤占可服务请求。\n\n## 诊断案例：三级为何比两级慢\n\n设已经确认两候选产生相同容许误差的输出，输入、tile、线程映射、计时边界与预热条件相同，仅改变缓冲级数。在目标机测得三级更慢，至少要区分下表的竞争解释。下表是待实施的实验推理，不是本库已经测得的GPU结果。\n\n| 解释 | 可区分的预测 | 保持可比的干预与观测 | 什么结果要求修正解释 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 首尾比重增加 | 长K时固定额外代价被摊薄，稳态斜率应更好或接近；J很小时差距大 | 固定tile与实现，扫J，拟合固定项和每阶段项；逐次计时而非只计峰值吞吐 | 三级的长J斜率更坏，不能再只归因首尾 |\n| 寄存器压力造成spill | 三级新增local读写，寄存器/片段生存区间改变，长J也持续支付交通 | 检查实际生成程序和local交通；缩小非关键临时量或改变其生存区间，再以相同计算验证 | 未出现spill或消除spill后仍不改善，需要其他解释；“寄存器数高”本身不等于spill |\n| 驻留降低 | 单CTA三级可能更快，足够CTA的整网格反而慢；共享/寄存器限制对应驻留台阶 | 分开比较单CTA与多CTA，记录实际资源和活跃CTA；只改变可用资源或tile时重新记录工作量 | 驻留不变且未受资源限制时，这个解释缺少支持 |\n| 没有形成预期重叠 | 源程序增加异步表达，实际复制/矩阵指令或等待位置却未改善，暴露空洞仍在 | 查生成指令与依赖，使用时间线/受限微实验；保持工作量检查copy与compute是否能独立推进 | 等待确实减少但运行变慢，应调查新增交通/驻留，不能仍称“没有重叠” |\n| 准备或转换增加 | 只看kernel可能改善，完整调用却变慢；重复复用准备后差距应改变 | 分别记录准备、转换、kernel和输出可消费时刻，按真实复用频率比较 | 差距全在kernel内时，准备不能解释它 |\n\n给一个可复算的反驳过程。若两级时间模型为T₂=10+2J，三级为T₃=18+1.7J，单位均τ，则J=8时26对31.6、J=64时138对126.8，交点约26.67。“小输入三级慢主要因为首尾”与这组设定一致。随后若新增长输入观测却符合T₃′=18+2.4J，J=64时为171.6，则更大的固定项已经不足以解释，必须调查稳态瓶颈。所有值为合成观测，不能放入运行观测库冒充设备测量。\n\n若再发现三级共享分配超过某个驻留台阶、local交通没有增加，应把“spill导致更慢”的假设降级，优先检验资源驻留；但驻留和未重叠可以同时发生，消除一个因素后仍需复测完整边界。只降低寄存器上限可能诱发更多spill，不是隔离驻留的干净实验。改变tile也同时改变复用、任务数和算术强度，应明确这些混杂，而非把每次变快都归因于唯一指标。差异落在重复测量的不确定区间时保留未知，方法见[统计实验](../tools/statistical-experiments.md)。\n\n正确性诊断使用与性能假设分开的反例：K=0/1/7/8/9/17，M/N非整除，交换两warp结束次序，延迟最后一次复制；用独立稠密参考检查每个输出，再注入“只等一个读者”“把旧代票据当新代”“少消费最后阶段”“将全局stride误用为共享stride”。先确认这些错误确实可被检查发现，才讨论流水更快。标量参考与有限事件模型不能证明真实GPU的作用域、可见性或内存竞态均正确。\n\n## 流水的首尾与短输入必须单独推导\n\n固定 [FA3 v1](../sources/S-FLASHATTENTION3.md) 的多级伪代码展示了重叠意图，但打印的循环边界和部分状态交接不足以直接作为实现。例如两阶段示意在只有两块 K/V 时，需要明确计算第二块分数，并依次使用两块各自的概率与 V；不能让循环为空后仍引用未生成的最后概率。独立时序表应保证每个必要 K/V 块恰好计算一次、输出使用同一最大值尺度、收尾按最终归一化分母处理，并覆盖 1、2、3 块、非整除尾部和取消前的最后访问。\n\n其三阶段实验也说明，更多理论重叠可能因额外概率片段和缩放状态挤占寄存器，迫使 tile 变小；实际编译结果又未得到预期的全部重叠。因此候选选择应同时比较算法依赖、生成指令和资源占用，不能只比较源代码里的阶段数。\n\n## 没有 TMA 与线程块簇时的流水组织\n\n固定 CUDA 12.8.0 编程指南的特性表将TMA单元、线程块簇与分布式共享存储支持的边界列为CC 9.0，将所述global→shared硬件加速异步复制与硬件split arrive/wait barrier的边界列为CC 8.0。这里说的是所列特性的硬件支持，不能由没有硬件加速推出不存在软件同步。本章的生产者/消费者与复用依赖也不要求某一代专有指令。\n\n同为8.x也不能共用一组容量。固定指南Table 24列出下列值；其中KB按1024字节解释。它们是规格上限，不保证编译出的kernel达到该驻留，较大动态共享分配还需满足相应配置与opt-in条件。\n\n| CC | 每SM线程/warp上限 | 每SM共享上限 | 每block共享上限 |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| 8.0 | 2048 / 64 | 164KiB | 163KiB |\n| 8.6 | 1536 / 48 | 100KiB | 99KiB |\n| 8.7 | 1536 / 48 | 164KiB | 163KiB |\n| 8.9 | 1536 / 48 | 100KiB | 99KiB |\n| 9.0 | 2048 / 64 | 228KiB | 227KiB |\n\n全局到共享存储的加载：没有TMA时，可以由线程计算地址，通过普通加载或可用的异步复制指令搬运。对齐、swizzle和索引可能增加发射与寄存器需求。cp.async可使用4/8/16字节片段，按所用接口的批次/同步对象等候；TMA处理多维描述符与事务完成时有不同契约。一次tile复制不等于物理上只有一笔事务，任何路径都应区分描述符数、发出指令与实际存储事务。\n\n跨block的部分和交换：选择不同CTA各算一段归约时，常规独立CTA实现可经设备全局存储写部分结果，再由后续kernel或合适的原子协议合并；这会增加交通与完成边界。存在具备其自身launch/驻留约束的协作执行等其他选择，不能断言无簇时所有组织都只能采用某一协议。另一选择是不拆到多个CTA，直接避免跨CTA部分和，但可能留下并行度不足。真实收益取决于额外交通与所缓解的瓶颈。\n\n片上容量：三级32KiB输入缓冲已占96KiB，在99KiB每block上限下只剩3KiB给其他共享对象，不能再增加完整32KiB一级；在227KiB上容量约束较松，但多分配仍可能降低每SM驻留。缩小Kₜ或减少级数是候选，改变线程数、累加放置与tile则要一起重算寄存器和共享账。延迟隐藏可能来自更多可运行warp、每线程独立工作或异步重叠，不能由CC标签直接判定哪一种占主导。\n\n矩阵指令：固定指南§16.7–16.8区分CC 8.0/8.6/8.7的第三代与8.9/9.0的第四代Tensor Core及其输入格式支持；9.0另有warpgroup范围的异步矩阵机制。具体形状、dtype、布局与工具链入口仍按目标指令核实，不能从“支持FP8”推出任何矩阵API都支持FP8。把Hopper的warpgroup分工改到warp级指令，需要重新安排accumulator持有者、交接与完成范围；改到ROCm的MFMA/LDS或其他平台同理，不能继承32lane与CUDA barrier约定。\n\n这些替代各自的成本可以单独测量：加载指令占比、cp.async 等待空洞、全局交换字节与归约 kernel 时间、占用率上限下的实际驻留。它们是设计轴上的点，不是 8.x 的固定配方；一个在 9.0 上最优的流水在 8.9 上可能被更浅、更小、更多 block 的组织超过。轴上未列点同样合法。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance IO-Awareness](../sources/S-FLASH.md)：FlashAttention 通过片上分块和", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 4500], [8000, 12000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 14078, "selected_characters": 8500, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "knowledge/tools/statistical-experiments.md", "sha256": "908b3588a6fa20bb1f2aed9f5db51c4b462953d79f8944cdbcfea7141c74644f", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-TOOLS-007：性能实验：独立重复、效应量与尾部证据\n\n性能结论要同时回答改了多少、波动来自哪里，以及结论适用于哪组条件。更多计时样本只有在增加了与问题相关的信息时才提高可信度；同一进程内反复测量不能自动替代重启、重新构建或不同时间段的重复。\n\n## 先固定比较的对象\n\n声明比较的是冷启动、稳态单请求、固定到达率服务，还是容量边界。版本、硬件与配额、模型、输入/输出分布、缓存条件、质量标准和测量事件必须一致。把预打包移出计时是可以研究的稳态设定，但需要另报一次性成本；它不能直接支持冷启动更快的结论。\n\n对于越小越好的延迟，既可以报告差值 A−B，也可以报告比值 A/B，但要说明用的是均值、分位数还是每轮比值的聚合。均值的比值、比值的均值与分位数的比值不是同一估计量。吞吐也要包含完成窗口、失败和未完成工作，而不是只选快速成功的请求。\n\n## 重复的层级\n\n一次实验包含环境/时间段、进程或模型实例、请求/迭代等层级。布局、编译、NUMA、热状态和后台竞争可以在不同层级引入变化。[效应量置信区间研究](../sources/S-STATS.md) §6–7 将这种层级变化与重复次数、比值区间连接起来；不能把其假设之外的任意轨迹直接代入公式。\n\n如果只有进程内反复采样，而进程间差异很大，继续增加内层重复会得到看似稳定但覆盖范围很窄的均值。先用少量分层试验辨别主要变异，再把预算投到有效层级。稳态预热规则需要事先确定或用独立预实验建立；若系统始终存在周期和漂移，应报告这种行为，而非挑一段最快区间称为稳态。\n\n## 配对、区组与区间\n\n对可重复的请求轨迹，可以在同一个时间区组内运行 A/B，并随机或均衡安排先后，控制环境漂移。配对的单位应是独立运行或区组，而不是把同一运行里强相关的每个 token 当作独立样本。若两方案会改变后续生成内容，应区分固定前缀计算对照与真实生成质量/性能对照。\n\n在独立配对差值 dⱼ 近似满足相应 t 区间条件时，均值差的区间为 d̄±t·s𝒹/√n。设定 16 个独立区组，平均降低 2 ms、差值样本标准差为 1 ms，使用自由度 15 的双侧 95% 临界值约 2.131，区间约为 [1.467,2.533] ms。这是给定统计量的算例；它既不证明某实现有该收益，也不是“未来单请求延迟落在其中的概率”。\n\n层级 bootstrap 应保留实际层级与配对关系。时间相关的数据需要考虑成块重采样或更高层独立重复，不能随机打散相关结构后制造很窄的区间。少量独立块、强漂移、极端长尾会限制区间可信度；无论用解析方法还是重采样，都需要说明独立性、平稳性与样本范围。\n\n## 尾延迟与失败不能被均值掩盖\n\np99 依赖尾部的少量样本。100 个请求的经验 p99 只有很有限的尾部信息，重复报告多个小数位不会增加精度。超时是被删失或失败的观测，不应悄悄删除；如果 SLO 定义要求在 deadline 内成功，超时和质量失败应按该定义计入。\n\n对固定阈值的超限率，假设 n 个独立、同分布请求且观察到零超限，单侧置信度 1−α 的上界为 1−α^(1/n)，来自零超限概率 (1−q)ⁿ。n=100、α=0.05 时上界约 2.95%；所以零失败 100 次并不能支持真实超限率低于 1% 的同等置信结论。这个二项式算例在突发相关、负载漂移或选择性停止时不能照搬。\n\n服务试验还应展示 offered load、队列增长、完成数与错误数。有限时间内的 goodput 不代表长期稳定；若队列持续增长，测量窗口结束时尚未完成的请求是关键证据。\n\n## 搜索后的验证与停止条件\n\n从很多候选中挑最好的结果，会同时挑中有利噪声。探索用的轨迹、形状与调参集应与最终保留验证分开；确定候选后冻结代码和配置，在独立运行上重测，并报告试过的候选范围。失败的正确性检查不能因为性能好而被排除。\n\n停止条件围绕实际决策设定：例如在质量通过且 SLO 不退化的前提下，收益是否超过值得维护的最小幅度。区间跨过决策边界时，证据不足以决定；应增加有效重复或说明未区分，而不是把单次最好值当成结论。持续查看结果直到恰好显著再停止，会改变统计含义，需要预先设计或使用相应的序贯方法。\n\n发布结论保留原始事件、运行身份、失败样本、聚合定义及不确定性。一次局部 kernel 对照仅支持该 shape 与条件；知识库是否提高 Agent 设计能力，还需要固定任务、模型和预算，对比旧 base、扩充 base 与无知识条件，并隔离各自运行学习。\n\n## 针对知识库作用安排受控构建实验\n\n下面是研究者可选择的对照设计，统计重复原则受上述固定来源支持，具体 Agent 条件不归称为来源论文的实验，也不构成 Agent 构建时必须执行的流程。研究知识条件差异时，可比较无知识、旧 seed、扩充 seed；研究运行学习时，可以另设 learned 条件。每组固定实际角色模型、任务语义、可用工具、基础代码起点、外部资料权限和预算口径，并在批次开始前冻结知识版本与评价器。各组拥有独立的案例与发布状态，防止先运行的组把经验传给后运行的组。\n\n主要结果包括固定预算内完整引擎的成功交付率、到第一个合格系统的成本、到目标性能的成本、以及合格系统的端到端表现。固定预算完成率中，未交付均计为未成功并保留原因。若另分析到达合格系统的时间，仍可能完成、且仅因预先统一的时间窗口结束而停止的运行，可以记为行政性右删失；崩溃或不可恢复失败应另列失败事件，不能任意按独立删失处理。其他资源预算导致的停止也需解释删失机制。不能只平均成功任务的完成时间而不说明选择范围。模型生成存在随机性，独立重复的单位是完整构建运行，而不是一个运行中的多个工作项。\n\n检索命中、正文读取和知识使用声明用于检查访问链路；预期设计改变是否进入实现，需要单独核对。若扩充组减少无效实验且在相同验收标准下实测总成本或到达合格系统的成本下降，即使未提高最终吞吐，也可以支持构建成本改善；仅减少实验次数并不充分，因为剩余实验可能更昂贵。若交付率提高却消耗更多资源，则应展示相应权衡，不能只报告最好的一条轨迹。\n\n扩充内容中的示范任务与算例不应直接成为唯一最终测试集。开发场景与独立保留场景分开，按模型结构、硬件能力、负载和状态交互说明哪些条件发生了变化。硬件不能全部交叉时报告实际测试子集，避免将一个设备的结果外推为跨平台收益。\n\n## 检查测量链是否会接受不可能的提升\n\n性能测量的负对照保持任务标准不变，主动制造应该使结果失格的轨迹：缺失响应却缩短总时间、提前结束测量仍有在途请求、只保留快请求、在 benchmark 中跳过真实模型路径。测量应拒绝这些轨迹或明确记录缺失/失败，而不是给出更好的成功分数。\n\n计量完成点与质量完成点应绑定到同一批请求和候选版本。profiling 的同步、额外复制或影子参考会扰动执行；用于诊断的计数与最终正常路径的性能分开报告。原始数据若不足以重建规定的统计量，结论是未测得，不以另一个聚合量替代。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-STATS：Quantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Confidence Intervals](../sources/S-STATS.md)：§6–7 连接多层随机影响、实验重复、效应量和置信区间，并明确方法假设。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-STATS/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-STATS/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-MLPERF-SCENARIOS：MLCommons — Inference Mobile Scenarios](../sources/S-MLPERF-SCENARIOS.md)：不同推理场景使用不同请求驱动方式，例如前一响应完成后再发与独立到达。相同平均吞吐并不表示相同负载压力。\n  [算法与机制说明](../sources/S-MLPERF-SCENARIOS.md)\n\n- "knowledge/hardware/memory-and-roofline.md", "sha256": "2bebeecbaa625667b8ad8c2fbc13449f0da31ac24b0abd6289127be1150c7d0c", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-HW-001：容量、算术强度与服务 Roofline\n\n容量决定系统能容纳哪些状态，带宽与计算能力决定搬运和处理这些状态的下界，关键路径决定哪些下界实际显现。\n\n## 三本不同的账\n\n容量账是权重＋持久状态＋激活峰值＋workspace＋图池＋通信缓冲＋分配碎片。流量账是一次服务实际跨某层内存边界读写的字节。计算账是该次服务的算术工作。一个对象驻留一次，可以在多个 token 步中被反复读取；分配字节不能直接当作总访存流量。\n\n对工作量 F FLOPs、边界流量 D 字节、有效算力 P 和带宽 B，理想时间下界为 max(F/P,D/B)，算术强度 I＝F/D，屋顶吞吐为 min(P,BI)。这假定计算与搬运可充分重叠，实际还可能受 launch、依赖、占用率和尾部不均衡限制。使用可持续有效带宽比峰值广告参数更接近服务路径。\n\n## 权重与 KV 谁主导\n\n小 batch dense decode 中，权重可能每步读取较大部分，多个请求共用权重可摊薄每输出 token 成本。KV 则主要按各请求历史长度读取。batch 增大降低权重/token 项，却增加总 KV 和激活，使瓶颈可能从权重转向 attention。长上下文改变这两个项的交点。\n\n假设一轮需要读 8 GiB 权重和 2 GiB KV，理想有效带宽 1 TiB/s，则这部分流量下界约 9.77 ms；并发 16 个 decode 请求时，单位输出 token 约摊到 0.61 ms，但每个请求仍等待整轮。吞吐和单请求延迟因此不能用同一个除法解释。\n\n## 内存层级改变 D\n\n寄存器、片上共享存储、L2 和设备主存各有不同带宽和容量。某层复用减少其上游流量，可能增加寄存器压力并降低 occupancy。attention tiling 减少 HBM 中间量，量化减少表示字节，前缀共享减少重复状态，三者作用于不同项，可组合却不能把加速比直接相乘。\n\n容量瓶颈也会转化为时间：KV 不足导致抢占与重算，主存溢出导致换页，过多图桶挤出可服务请求。选择算法前应先还原当前状态在哪一层驻留、在哪一边界重复传输。\n\n## 从局部模型回到完整服务\n\nAmdahl 关系说明，若被优化部分占总关键路径比例 f，局部加速 s，则理想总加速为 1/((1−f)＋f/s)。流水重叠时不能从各 kernel 累计时长直接得到 f，应从依赖图中真正延迟输出的区间估计。Roofline 用来形成可解释的候选方向，再由时间线决定当前哪个资源确实卡住。\n\n## 给每条资源边界单独记账\n\n一份可组合的资源模型列出 F、D寄存器/片上、D设备主存、D主存、D主机设备、D网络、D存储，以及 CPU 服务时间、提交次数和同步事件。字节按实际跨边界的方向计数：权重已在 HBM 驻留，不代表每步不再从 HBM 读取；本地 KV 共享减少存储副本，也不一定消除各请求的 attention 读取。\n\n理想资源下界可以写为各项需求除以可用能力后的最大值，但前提是这些资源能够独立重叠。若 SSD 读取、网络接收和 GPU staging 共用内存控制器或 PCIe 上游链路，就要对共享资源的流量求和；若两个阶段有顺序依赖，就要沿关键路径累加。把每条链路峰值相加会高估实际吞吐。\n\n设定每次迭代主机到设备搬运 1 GiB，主机链路有效带宽 16 GiB/s；设备内部另读 10 GiB，设备带宽 1 TiB/s。两个理想时间分别为 62.5 ms 与 9.77 ms。若原本只优化后者，最多省下的区间受关键路径限制；若传输和计算完全不重叠，二者要相加。这个例子解释为什么卸载问题不能只用 HBM roofline 判断。\n\n## 怎样得到可持续参数\n\n带宽测量匹配访问方式：顺序读、随机读、读写混合、消息大小、队列深度、NUMA 位置和并发消费者都会改变结果。矩阵能力也与 dtype、shape、布局、稀疏条件和 kernel 路径有关。先测一组能够覆盖目标形态的微基准，再用完整服务校准，保留测量条件和范围，而不是选一个最高数值用于所有请求。逐项测量条件、常见误读与参数包的组织见 [GPU 从零表征](../tools/gpu-characterization.md)；CPU 侧见 [CPU 推理](cpu-serving.md) 的能力表一节。\n\n模型预测与实际时间的差值可拆成重复流量、同步/启动、资源争用、负载不均和排队；需要用观测区分。若总线计数不支持预测的字节下降，即使最终速度提高，也应重新解释机制。多个优化一起加入时，单项节省可能重叠，不能重复抵扣同一笔流量。\n\n## 成本、能量与边界变化\n\n实例租用成本包含空闲、加载和排空时间；能量是所选测量边界内功率随时间的积分。设备功率、整机功率和机房口径不同，不能拼成同一数字。单位有效请求成本为总计费成本除以满足质量与 SLO 的请求数；失败、重算和未完成工作仍消耗资源，不应从分子删除。\n\n提高 batch 可能降低每 token 权重流量和能量，却延长用户等待；低位权重可能减小驻留量，却增加解包 CPU 时间。目标从峰值吞吐切换到交互 SLO、功率上限或成本预算时，最优运行点会变化。资源账帮助排除不可行方案，实际选择仍要落在满足质量和服务约束的区域。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-CUDA-BEST：CUDA C++ Best Practices Guide](../sources/S-CUDA-BEST.md)：CUDA 性能资料区分有效带宽、访存组织、数据传输和执行资源。合并访问、复用和减少不必要搬运会改变实际流量。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-CUDA-BEST/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-CUDA-BEST/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-PAGED：Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention](../sources/S-PAGED.md)：PagedAttention 把每条序列的逻辑 KV 划分为固定 token 数的块，用映射表访问不连续物理存储。多个序列可以共享相同前缀的物理块，分叉写入需要保持各序列状态独立。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-PAGED/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-PAGED/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-FLASH：FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness](../sources/S-FLASH.md)：FlashAttention 通过片上分块和在线 softmax，避免将完整 attention 分数与概率矩阵写入设备主存；目标函数仍是精确 attention 的数学形式。\n "knowledge/system-semantics/known-tensions.md", "sha256": "e7e30a27ff5b42d93b55bc6e36a008ce6f5c100410fbe357ee3870a4ad601f90", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-SYS-025：已知张力：单实例交互式服务中的机制冲突\n\n单实例交互式生成服务里，若干机制在同一资源与同一时延预算上相互拉扯。下列条目是有来源支撑的探索起点：各写机制、来源指针、可观测信号与反转条件。清单不穷尽，也不构成任务必须处理的缺陷列表。\n\n## Decode 带宽下界与 ITL\n\n机制：每轮 decode 若读取大块权重与各请求可见 KV，则设备主存流量给出迭代时间下界；客户端 ITL 还要覆盖主机、采样、同步与排队。当流量下界已接近或超过目标 ITL，继续堆叠同轮工作只会恶化间隔，吞吐与单请求间隔不能用同一除法解释。\n\n来源指针：[容量与 roofline](../hardware/memory-and-roofline.md) 中权重/KV 流量分解；[ORCA](../sources/S-ORCA.md) 的迭代批语义说明一轮推进多个请求仍共享同轮等待。\n\n可观测：活跃 decode 数增加时，每请求输出间隔近似不降或上升，而设备 HBM 字节/迭代接近表征带宽×时间；主机空闲比例高。\n\n反转：权重驻留被更高层缓存命中、KV 读取因共享或压缩真实下降、目标 ITL 放宽，或阶段分离使本实例不再承担该流量形态时，张力缓解或形态改变。\n\n## Prefill 与 Decode 干扰\n\n机制：长 prefill 的大矩阵与长上下文 attention 占用 SM、HBM 与工作区；与短 query decode 同实例交错时，decode 的迭代被拉长，表现为已有流式用户的 ITL 尖峰。分块把 prefill 切成可调度片，但每块仍须按可见性读取其依赖的历史，块长受剩余 ITL 预算约束，不是任意变小就无代价。\n\n来源指针：[SARATHI](../sources/S-SARATHI.md)；[分块 Prefill](../algorithms/chunked-prefill.md)。\n\n可观测：新长大请求到达后，无关会话的 token 间隔同步变差；时间线上出现长 prefill kernel 与 decode 交替，decode 间隙被拉宽；TTFT 改善若来自抢占 decode，常伴随 ITL 恶化。\n\n反转：prefill/decode 分实例且状态迁移成本可接受、到达以短输入为主、或时间片与准入使 decode 预算硬隔离时，同实例干扰下降。分实例引入的传输与调度是另一组成本。\n\n## 投机收益随 Batch 增大而消失\n\n机制：投机用草稿成本换目标并行验证；单位已确认 token 成本须低于普通目标 decode。连续批摊薄每 token 权重读取后，普通 decode 变便宜，同一 γ 与草稿配置可能从加速变为减速；额外验证 token 还占用其他请求预算。\n\n来源指针：[投机解码](../algorithms/speculation-budget.md)；[S-SPEC](../sources/S-SPEC.md)。设定算例见该章：普通每 token 10 单位、投机后 8 单位有益；普通降至 6 单位后同配置变慢。\n\n可观测：小批或单请求加速比明显，批增大后接受长度未变但端到端 token/s 低于关闭投机；验证占用的 token 预算挤出其他就绪请求。\n\n反转：批变小、草稿更准或更便宜、验证与 decode 能重叠且不挤占关键资源，或普通路径因别的原因变慢时，投机可再次有利。关闭投机是合法运行点。\n\n## KV 量化的质量悬崖\n\n机制：降低 KV 位宽削减驻留与部分流量，但误差经注意力传播；分组轴、尺度与残余区配置在某些位宽/模型/任务上出现断崖式质量下降，而不是平滑可插值的曲线。容量账按理想位宽估算会忽略 scale、残余与 workspace。\n\n来源指针：[KIVI](../sources/S-KIVI.md) §3–4 与适用边界；[量化契约](../algorithms/quantization-contract.md)。\n\n可观测：某位宽下任务指标相对基线突然恶化，相邻更高位宽仍接近基线；仅测 TTFT/首 token 正常，长生成后偏离；消费压缩历史的 decode 与仍用高精度缓存的 prefill 结果不一致。\n\n反转：换分组轴或残余策略、提高关键层精度、任务对误差不敏感，或长度/批使流量不再主导时，可重新评估。悬崖位置随模型与任务移动，不能当跨模型常数。\n\n## 图桶、Workspace 与 KV 争容量\n\n机制：CUDA Graph 与固定计划缓冲需要稳定地址与多桶驻留；注意力/矩阵 workspace、流水双缓冲同样占用设备容量。它们与 KV 争同一显存池：桶与 workspace 增大可降提交与峰值临时，却直接减少可驻留历史或并发。\n\n来源指针：[CUDA Graph 缓冲](../frameworks/cuda-graph-buffers.md)；[内存分配器](../system-semantics/memory-allocators.md)；[S-TORCH-CUDA](../sources/S-TORCH-CUDA.md)。\n\n可观测：启用更多图桶或更大 workspace 后，最大并发或最大上下文下降；OOM/驱逐与“计算变快”同时出现；页回收成功但图仍引用旧代次导致错误。\n\n反转：形状集合收窄使桶数下降、改为 eager 且提交不是关键路径、或扩容设备内存时，争用缓和。地址不稳定的负载下图路径本身可能先失效。\n\n## 小 Batch 下主机侧占比\n\n机制：设备迭代很短时，分词、调度、采样、JSON/协议与 launch 的主机时间在端到端中占比上升。优化仅针对 HBM 或 GEMM 时，壁钟改善受 Amdahl 限制；图与异步可摊薄提交，但不消除采样与协议成本。\n\n来源指针：[主机运行时](../system-semantics/host-runtime.md)；[CUDA 执行中的 eager/图](../hardware/cuda-execution.md)；[S-NSYS](../sources/S-NSYS.md) 对 host/device 时间线边界的说明。\n\n可观测：设备利用率低但 ITL 仍高；时间线显示 kernel 很短、host 线程或运行时跨度占间隔大部分；增大批后设备占比上升、主机占比下降。\n\n反转：批变大、输出更长使设备工作占优，或主机路径与设备重叠且无假同步时，张力形态改变。主机变快不自动提高已饱和的 HBM 路径。\n\n## 张力之间的耦合\n\n上述条目常耦合：为缓解带宽–ITL 而增大批，可能削弱投机；为保护 ITL 而切细 prefill，可能增加 launch 与主机占比；为省 KV 而量化，可能撞上质量悬崖；为降提交而加图桶，可能挤掉并发。要区分单因素效应，可以控制其他条件；要识别交互作用，则比较交叉配置或建立因素实验。改动多个机制本身合法，关键是证据能否区分各解释，不能把组合效应任意归给一项。\n\n设计轴包括：同实例混合 vs 阶段分离、批大小与准入、投机开关与 γ、KV 表示位宽、图/eager、主机/设备重叠程度。轴上未列点同样合法。\n\n## 用成本关系判断张力是否真正成立\n\n设已确认某轮必须跨设备主存读取 D=8 GiB，匹配访问形态的可持续带宽 B=0.8 TiB/s，则只计该流量的时间下界为 D/B=9.765625 ms。若本例要求这一轮完成才能生成下一个 token，且 ITL 预算为 5 ms，这个运行点不能满足预算。以上均为设定值；张量的逻辑大小不能未经计量直接当作 D，cache 命中、共享读取和压缩都可能改变实际跨边界字节。\n\n若有人将延迟归因为提交空隙，另一解释是主存流量，区分方法是保持语义与长度不变，分别改变提交组织和读取字节，记录设备事务、关键路径与完整 token 间隔。图重放降低 host 时间而设备流量和 ITL 几乎不变，支持流量主导；减少合法读取量后设备时间下降，进一步支持这个解释。干扰、频率、并发和误差也要记录；单一利用率不能排除竞争解释。\n\n这个下界约束该运行点，不规定分离服务、量化、改变批或专用 kernel 中的哪一个必须采用。若优化只是重叠同一个共享带宽上的消费者，D/B 的总流量账不会因此消失。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## "d7ead62158a73d4a9ecff58495f901feba52a48f521313ce34fe0e0191c69d79", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:41497]\n--- PAGE 1 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nGPTQ: ACCURATE POST-TRAINING QUANTIZATION\nFOR GENERATIVE PRE-TRAINED TRANSFORMERS\nElias Frantar∗\nIST Austria\nSaleh Ashkboos\nETH Zurich\nTorsten Hoeﬂer\nETH Zurich\nDan Alistarh\nIST Austria & NeuralMagic\nABSTRACT\nGenerative Pre-trained Transformer models, known as GPT or OPT, set them-\nselves apart through breakthrough performance across complex language mod-\nelling tasks, but also by their extremely high computational and storage costs.\nSpeciﬁcally, due to their massive size, even inference for large, highly-accurate\nGPT models may require multiple performant GPUs, which limits the usability\nof such models. While there is emerging work on relieving this pressure via\nmodel compression, the applicability and performance of existing compression\ntechniques is limited by the scale and complexity of GPT models. In this paper,\nwe address this challenge, and propose GPTQ, a new one-shot weight quantiza-\ntion method based on approximate second-order information, that is both highly-\naccurate and highly-efﬁcient. Speciﬁcally, GPTQ can quantize GPT models with\n175 billion parameters in approximately four GPU hours, reducing the bitwidth\ndown to 3 or 4 bits per weight, with negligible accuracy degradation relative to the\nuncompressed baseline. Our method more than doubles the compression gains rel-\native to previously-proposed one-shot quantization methods, preserving accuracy,\nallowing us for the ﬁrst time to execute an 175 billion-parameter model inside a\nsingle GPU for generative inference. Moreover, we also show that our method\ncan still provide reasonable accuracy in the extreme quantization regime, in which\nweights are quantized to 2-bit or even ternary quantization levels. We show ex-\nperimentally that these improvements can be leveraged for end-to-end inference\nspeedups over FP16, of around 3.25x when using high-end GPUs (NVIDIA A100)\nand 4.5x when using more cost-effective ones (NVIDIA A6000). The implemen-\ntation is available at https://github.com/IST-DASLab/gptq.\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nPre-trained generative models from the Transformer (Vaswani et al., 2017) family, commonly known\nas GPT or OPT (Radford et al., 2019; Brown et al., 2020; Zhang et al., 2022), have shown break-\nthrough performance for complex language modelling tasks, leading to massive academic and prac-\ntical interest. One major obstacle to their usability is computational and storage cost, which ranks\namong the highest for known models. For instance, the best-performing model variants, e.g. GPT3-\n175B, have in the order of 175 billion parameters and require tens-to-hundreds of GPU years to\ntrain (Zhang et al., 2022). Even the simpler task of inferencing over a pre-trained model, which is\nour focus in this paper, is highly challenging: for instance, the parameters of GPT3-175B occupy\n326GB (counting in multiples of 1024) of memory when stored in a compact ﬂoat16 format. This\nexceeds the capacity of even the highest-end single GPUs, and thus inference must be performed\nusing more complex and expensive setups, such as multi-GPU deployments.\nAlthough a standard approach to eliminating these overheads is model compression, e.g. (Hoeﬂer\net al., 2021; Gholami et al., 2021), surprisingly little is known about compressing such models for\ninference. One reason is that more complex methods for low-bitwidth quantization or model prun-\ning usually require model retraining, which is extremely expensive for billion-parameter models.\nAlternatively, post-training methods (Nagel et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2020; Hubara et al., 2020;\nNahshan et al., 2021), which compress the model in one shot, without retraining, would be very\nappealing. Unfortunately, the more accurate variants of such methods (Li et al., 2021; Hubara et al.,\n2021; Frantar et al., 2022) are complex and challenging to scale to billions of parameters (Yao et al.,\n∗Corresponding author: elias.frantar@ist.ac.at\n1\narXiv:2210.17323v2  [cs.LG]  22 Mar 2023\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\n2022). To date, only basic variants of round-to-nearest quantization (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers\net al., 2022) have been applied at the scale of GPT-175B; while this works well for low compression\ntargets, e.g., 8-bit weights, they fail to preserve accuracy at higher rates. It therefore remains open\nwhether one-shot post-training quantization to higher compression rates is generally-feasible.\n10\n1\n100\n101\n102\n#params in billions\n5\n10\n15\n20\n25\n30\n35\n40\n45\n50\nPerplexity on WikiText2\n110.\nOPT Model Family\n4bit RTN\n4bit GPTQ\nFP16\n100\n101\n102\n#params in billions\n10\n20\n30\n40\n50\n60\nPerplexity on WikiText2\n571.\nBLOOM Model Family\n3bit RTN\n3bit GPTQ\nFP16\nFigure 1: Quantizing OPT models to 4 and BLOOM models to 3 bit precision, comparing GPTQ\nwith the FP16 baseline and round-to-nearest (RTN) (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022).\nContribution. In this paper, we present a new post-training quantization method, called GPTQ,1\nwhich is efﬁcient enough to execute on models with hundreds of billions of parameters in at most\na few hours, and precise enough to compress such models to 3 or 4 bits per parameter without\nsigniﬁcant loss of accuracy. For illustration, GPTQ can quantize the largest publicly-available mod-\nels, OPT-175B and BLOOM-176B, in approximately four GPU hours, with minimal increase in\nperplexity, known to be a very stringent accuracy metric.\nFurther, we show that our model can also provide robust results in the extreme quantization regime,\nin which models are quantized to 2 bits per component, or even ternary values. On the practical\nside, we develop an execution harness which allows us to execute the resulting compressed models\nefﬁciently for generative tasks. Speciﬁcally, we are able to run the compressed OPT-175B model\nfor the ﬁrst time on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU, or using only two more cost-effective NVIDIA\nA6000 GPUs. We also implement bespoke GPU kernels which are able to leverage compression for\nfaster memory loading, resulting in speedups of ≈3.25× when using A100 GPUs, and 4.5× when\nusing A6000 GPUs.\nTo our knowledge, we are the ﬁrst to show that extremely accurate language models with hundreds\nof billions of parameters can be quantized to 3-4 bits/component: prior post-training methods only\nremain accurate at 8 bits (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022), while prior training-based tech-\nniques have only tackled models that are smaller by one to two orders of magnitude (Wu et al., 2022).\nThis high degree of compression may appear natural, as these networks are overparametrized; yet,\nas we discuss in our detailed analysis of results, compression induces non-trivial tradeoffs between\nthe accuracy of the language modeling (perplexity), bit-width, and the size of the original model.\nWe hope that our work will stimulate further research in this area, and can be a further step towards\nmaking these models available to a wider audience. In terms of limitations, our method currently\ndoes not provide speedups for the actual multiplications, due to the lack of hardware support for\nmixed-precision operands (e.g. FP16 x INT4) on mainstream architectures. Moreover, our current\nresults do not include activation quantization, as they are not a signiﬁcant bottleneck in our target\nscenarios; however, this can be supported using orthogonal techniques (Yao et al., 2022).\n2\nRELATED WORK\nQuantization methods fall broadly into two categories: quantization during training, and post-\ntraining methods. The former quantize models during typically extensive retraining and/or ﬁne-\ntuning, using some approximate differentiation mechanism for the rounding operation (Gholami\net al., 2021; Nagel et al., 2021). By contrast, post-training (“one-shot”) methods quantize a pre-\n1This merges the name of the OPT model family with the abbreviation for post-training quantization (PTQ).\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\ntrained model using modest resources, typically a few thousand data samples and a few hours of\ncomputation. Post-training approaches are particularly interesting for massive models, for which\nfull model training or even ﬁnetuning can be expensive. We focus on this scenario here.\nPost-training Quantization. Most post-training methods have focused on vision models. Usually,\naccurate methods operate by quantizing either individual layers, or small blocks of consecutive\nlayers. (See Section 3 for more details.) The AdaRound method (Nagel et al., 2020) computes a\ndata-dependent rounding by annealing a penalty term, which encourages weights to move towards\ngrid points corresponding to quantization levels. BitSplit (Wang et al., 2020) constructs quantized\nvalues bit-by-bit using a squared error objective on the residual error, while AdaQuant (Hubara et al.,\n2021) performs direct optimization based on straight-through estimates. BRECQ (Li et al., 2021)\nintroduces Fisher information into the objective, and optimizes layers within a single residual block\njointly. Finally, Optimal Brain Quantization (OBQ) (Frantar et al., 2022) generalizes the classic\nOptimal Brain Surgeon (OBS) second-order weight pruning framework (Hassibi et al., 1993; Singh\n& Alistarh, 2020; Frantar et al., 2021) to apply to quantization. OBQ quantizes weights one-by-one,\nin order of quantization error, always adjusting the remaining weights. While these approaches can\nproduce good results for models up to ≈100 million parameters in a few GPU hours, scaling them\nto networks orders of magnitude larger is challenging.\nLarge-model Quantization.\nWith the recent open-source releases of language models like\nBLOOM (Laurenc¸on et al., 2022) or OPT-175B (Zhang et al., 2022), researchers have started to\ndevelop affordable methods for compressing such giant networks for inference. While all exist-\ning works—ZeroQuant (Yao et al., 2022), LLM.int8() (Dettmers et al., 2022), and nuQmm (Park\net al., 2022)— carefully select quantization granularity, e.g., vector-wise, they ultimately just round\nweights to the nearest (RTN) quantization level, in order to maintain acceptable runtimes for very\nlarge models. ZeroQuant further proposes layer-wise knowledge distillation, similar to AdaQuant,\nbut the largest model it can apply this approach to has only 1.3 billion parameters. At this scale,\nZeroQuant already takes ≈3 hours of compute; GPTQ quantizes models 100× larger in ≈4 hours.\nLLM.int8() observes that activation outliers in a few feature dimensions break the quantization\nof larger models, and proposes to ﬁx this problem by keeping those dimensions in higher preci-\nsion. Lastly, nuQmm develops efﬁcient GPU kernels for a speciﬁc binary-coding based quantization\nscheme.\nRelative to this line of work, we show that a signiﬁcantly more complex and accurate quantizer can\nbe implemented efﬁciently at large model scale. Speciﬁcally, GPTQ more than doubles the amount\nof compression relative to these prior techniques, at similar accuracy.\n3\nBACKGROUND\nLayer-Wise Quantization. At a high level, our method follows the structure of state-of-the-art\npost-training quantization methods (Nagel et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2020; Hubara et al., 2021; Fran-\ntar et al., 2022), by performing quantization layer-by-layer, solving a corresponding reconstruction\nproblem for each layer. Concretely, let Wℓbe the weights corresponding to a linear layer ℓand let\nXℓdenote the layer input corresponding to a small set of m data points running through the network.\nThen, the objective is to ﬁnd a matrix of quantized weights c\nW which minimizes the squared error,\nrelative to the full precision layer output. Formally, this can be restated as\nargminc\nW ||WX −c\nWX||2\n2.\n(1)\nFurther, similar to (Nagel et al., 2020; Li et al., 2021; Frantar et al., 2022), we assume that the\nquantization grid for c\nW is ﬁxed before the process, and that individual weights can move freely as\nin (Hubara et al., 2021; Frantar et al., 2022).\nOptimal Brain Quantization.\nOur approach builds on the recently-proposed Optimal Brain\nQuanization (OBQ) method (Frantar et al., 2022) for solving the layer-wise quantization problem\ndeﬁned above, to which we perform a series of major modiﬁcations, which allow it to scale to large\nlanguage models, providing more than three orders of magnitude computational speedup. To aid\nunderstanding, we ﬁrst brieﬂy summarize the original OBQ method.\nThe OBQ method starts from the observation that Equation (1) can be written as the sum of the\nsquared errors, over each row of W. Then, OBQ handles each row w independently, quantizing one\nweight at a time while always updating all not-yet-quantized weights, in order to compensate for\nthe error incurred by quantizing a single weight. Since the corresponding objective is a quadratic,\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nwhose Hessian is HF = 2XF X⊤\nF , where F denotes the set of remaining full-precision weights,\nthe greedy-optimal weight to quantize next, which we denote by wq, and the corresponding optimal\nupdate of all weights in F, denoted by δF , are given by the following formulas, where quant(w)\nrounds w to the nearest value on the quantization grid:\nwq = argminwq\n(quant(wq) −wq)2\n[H−1\nF ]qq\n,\nδF = −wq −quant(wq)\n[H−1\nF ]qq\n· (H−1\nF ):,q.\n(2)\nOBQ quantizes weights iteratively using these two equations, until all the weights of w are quan-\ntized. This is done efﬁciently, avoiding expensive full recomputations of H−1, by removing the qth\nrow and column of H, which is necessary after quantizing wq, directly in the inverse via one step of\nGaussian elimination. Namely, the updated inverse is given by the formula\nH−1\n−q =\n\u0010\nH−1 −\n1\n[H−1]qq\nH−1\n:,q H−1\nq,:\n\u0011\n−p.\n(3)\nThis method comes with a vectorized implementation, handling multiple rows of W in parallel.\nEventually, the algorithm can achieve reasonable runtimes on medium-sized models: for instance, it\ncan fully quantize the ResNet-50 model (25M parameters) in ≈1 hour on a single GPU, which is\nroughly in line with other post-training methods achieving state-of-the-art accuracy (Frantar et al.,\n2022). However, the fact that OBQ’s runtime for a drow ×dcol matrix W has cubic input dependency\nO(drow · d3\ncol) means that applying it to models with billions of parameters is extremely expensive.\n4\nTHE GPTQ ALGORITHM\nStep 1: Arbitrary Order Insight. As explained in the previous section, OBQ quantizes weights in\ngreedy order, i.e. it always picks the weight which currently incurs the least additional quantization\nerror. Interestingly, we ﬁnd that, while this quite natural strategy does indeed seem to perform very\nwell, its improvement over quantizing the weights in arbitrary order is generally small, in particular\non large, heavily-parametrized layers. Most likely, this is because the slightly lower number of\nquantized weights with large individual error is balanced out by those weights being quantized\ntowards the end of the process, when only few other unquantized weights that can be adjusted for\ncompensation remain. As we will now discuss, this insight that any ﬁxed order may perform well,\nespecially on large models, has interesting ramiﬁcations.\nInverse Layer Hessian\n(Cholesky Form)\ncomputed initially\nblock i quantized recursively\ncolumn-by-column\nWeight Matrix / Block\nunquantized weights\nthat are updated\nquantized weights\nFigure 2: GPTQ quantization procedure. Blocks\nof consecutive columns (bolded) are quantized at\na given step, using the inverse Hessian informa-\ntion stored in the Cholesky decomposition, and\nthe remaining weights (blue) are updated at the\nend of the step. The quantization procedure is\napplied recursively inside each block: the white\nmiddle column is currently being quantized.\nThe original OBQ method quantizes rows of W\nindependently, in a speciﬁc order deﬁned by the\ncorresponding errors. By contrast, we will aim\nto quantize the weights of all rows in the same\norder, and will show that this typically yields\nresults with a ﬁnal squared error that is simi-\nlar to the original solutions. As a consequence,\nthe set of unquantized weights F and similarly\nH−1\nF\nis always the same for all rows (see Fig-\nure 2 for an illustration). In more detail, the lat-\nter is due to the fact that HF depends only on\nthe layer inputs XF , which are the same for all\nrows, and not on any weights. Therefore, we\nhave to perform the update of H−1\nF\ngiven by\nEquation (3) only dcol times, once per column,\nrather than drow·dcol times, once per weight. This\nreduces the overall runtime from O(drow · d3\ncol)\nto O(max {drow · d2\ncol, d3\ncol}), i.e., by a factor of\nmin {drow, dcol}. For larger models, this differ-\nence consists of several orders of magnitude.\nHowever, before this algorithm can actually be\napplied to very large models in practice, two ad-\nditional major problems need to be addressed.\nStep 2: Lazy Batch-Updates. First, a direct implementation of the scheme described previously\nwill not be fast in practice, because the algorithm has a relatively low compute-to-memory-access\nratio. For example, Equation (3) needs to update all elements of a potentially huge matrix using just a\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nfew FLOPs for each entry. Such operations cannot properly utilize the massive compute capabilities\nof modern GPUs, and will be bottlenecked by the signiﬁcantly lower memory bandwidth.\nFortunately, this problem can be resolved by the following observation: The ﬁnal rounding decisions\nfor column i are only affected by updates performed on this very column, and so updates to later\ncolumns are irrelevant at this point in the process. This makes it possible to “lazily batch” updates\ntogether, thus achieving much better GPU utilization. Concretely, we apply the algorithm to B =\n128 columns at a time, keeping updates contained to those columns and the corresponding B × B\nblock of H−1 (see also Figure 2). Only once a block has been fully processed, we perform global\nupdates of the entire H−1 and W matrices using the multi-weight versions of Equations (2) and\n(3) given below, with Q denoting a set of indices, and H−1\n−Q denoting the inverse matrix with the\ncorresponding rows and columns removed:\nδF = −(wQ −quant(wQ))([H−1\nF ]QQ)−1(H−1\nF ):,Q,\n(4)\nH−1\n−Q =\n\u0010\nH−1 −H−1\n:,Q([H−1]QQ)−1H−1\nQ,:\n\u0011\n−Q.\n(5)\nAlthough this strategy does not reduce the theoretical amount of compute, it effectively addresses\nthe memory-throughput bottleneck. This provides an order of magnitude speedup for very large\nmodels in practice, making it a critical component of our algorithm.\nStep 3: Cholesky Reformulation. The ﬁnal technical issue we have to address is given by numeri-\ncal inaccuracies, which can become a major problem at the scale of existing models, especially when\ncombined with the block updates discussed in the previous step. Speciﬁcally, it can occur that the\nmatrix H−1\nF becomes indeﬁnite, which we notice can cause the algorithm to aggressively update the\nremaining weights in incorrect directions, resulting in an arbitrarily-bad quantization of the corre-\nsponding layer. In practice, we observed that the probability of this happening increases with model\nsize: concretely, it almost certainly occurs for at least a few layers on models that are larger than\na few billion parameters. The main issue appears to be the repeated applications of Equation (5),\nwhich accumulate various numerical errors, especially through the additional matrix inversion.\nFor smaller models, applying dampening, that is adding a small constant λ (we always choose 1% of\nthe average diagonal value) to the diagonal elements of H appears to be sufﬁcient to avoid numerical\nissues. However, larger models require a more robust and general approach.\nTo address this, we begin by noting that the only information required from H−1\nFq , where Fq denotes\nthe set of unquantized weights when quantizing weight q, is row q, or more precisely, the elements in\nthis row starting with the diagonal. The consequence is that we could precompute all of these rows\nusing a more numerically-stable method without any signiﬁcant increase in memory consumption.\nIndeed, the row removal via (3) for our symmetric H−1 essentially corresponds to taking a Cholesky\ndecomposition, except for the minor difference that the latter divides row q by ([H−1\nFq ]qq)1/2. Hence,\nwe can leverage state-of-the-art Cholesky kernels to compute all information we will need from H−1\nupfront. In combination with mild dampening, the resulting method is robust enough to execute on\nhuge models without issues. As a bonus, using a well-optimized Cholesky kernel also yields further\nspeedup. We detail all small changes necessary for the Cholesky version of the algorithm next.\nThe Full Algorithm. Finally, we present the full pseudocode for GPTQ in Algorithm 1, including\nthe optimizations discussed above.\nAlgorithm 1 Quantize W given inverse Hessian H−1 = (2XX⊤+ λI)−1 and blocksize B.\nQ ←0drow×dcol\n// quantized output\nE ←0drow×B\n// block quantization errors\nH−1 ←Cholesky(H−1)⊤\n// Hessian inverse information\nfor i = 0, B, 2B, . . . do\nfor j = i, . . . , i + B −1 do\nQ:,j ←quant(W:,j)\n// quantize column\nE:,j−i ←(W:,j −Q:,j) / [H−1]jj\n// quantization error\nW:,j:(i+B) ←W:,j:(i+B) −E:,j−i · H−1\nj,j:(i+B)\n// update weights in block\nend for\nW:,(i+B): ←W:,(i+B): −E · H−1\ni:(i+B),(i+B):\n// update all remaining weights\nend for\n5\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\n5\nEXPERIMENTAL VALIDATION\nOverview. We begin our experiments by validating the accuracy of GPTQ relative to other accurate-\nbut-expensive quantizers, on smaller models, for which these methods provide reasonable runtimes.\nNext, we examine GPTQ’s runtime scaling for very large models. Then, we present 3- and 4-bit\nquantization results for the entire BLOOM and OPT model families, evaluated via perplexity on\nchallenging language generation tasks. In addition, we show that our method is also stable for 2-bit\nquantization when the granularity is reduced to small blocks of consecutive weights. To complement\nthis perplexity analysis, we also evaluate the resulting quantized models on a series of standard zero-\nshot tasks. Finally, we focus on the two largest (and interesting) openly-available models, Bloom-\n176B and OPT-175B, where we perform a detailed evaluation on several tasks. For these models, we\nalso present practical improvements, namely reducing the number of GPUs required for inference\nas well as end-to-end speedups for generative tasks.\nSetup. We implemented GPTQ in PyTorch (Paszke et al., 2019) and worked with the HuggingFace\nintegrations of the BLOOM (Laurenc¸on et al., 2022) and OPT (Zhang et al., 2022) model families.\nWe quantized all models (including the 175 billion parameter variants) using a single NVIDIA A100\nGPU with 80GB of memory. Our entire GPTQ calibration data consists of 128 random 2048 token\nsegments from the C4 dataset (Raffel et al., 2020), i.e., excerpts from randomly crawled websites,\nwhich represents generic text data. We emphasize that this means that GPTQ does not see any\ntask-speciﬁc data, and our results thus remain actually “zero-shot”. We perform standard uniform\nper-row asymmetric quantization on the min-max grid, similar to Dettmers et al. (2022). Additional\nevaluation details can be found in Appendix A.2.1.\nTo ensure that the entire compression procedure can be performed with signiﬁcantly less GPU mem-\nory than what would be required to run the full precision model, some care must be taken. Specif-\nically, we always load one Transformer block, consisting of 6 layers, at a time into GPU memory\nand then accumulate the layer-Hessians and perform quantization. Finally, the current block inputs\nare sent through the fully quantized block again to produce the new inputs for the quantization of\nthe next block. Hence, the quantization process operates not on the layer inputs in the full precision\nmodel but on the actual layer inputs in the already partially quantized one. We ﬁnd that this brings\nnoticeable improvements at negligible extra cost.\nBaselines. Our primary baseline, denoted by RTN, consists of rounding all weights to the nearest\nquantized value on exactly the same asymmetric per-row grid that is also used for GPTQ, meaning\nthat it corresponds precisely to the state-of-the-art weight quantization of LLM.int8(). This is cur-\nrently the method of choice in all works on quantization of very large language models (Dettmers\net al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Park et al., 2022): its runtime scales well to networks with many bil-\nlions of parameters, as it simply performs direct rounding. As we will also discuss further, more\naccurate methods, such as AdaRound (Nagel et al., 2020) or BRECQ (Li et al., 2021), are currently\ntoo slow for models with many billions of parameters, the main focus of this work. Nevertheless,\nwe also show that GPTQ is competitive with such methods for small models, while scaling to huge\nones like OPT-175B as well.\nQuantizing Small Models. As a ﬁrst ablation study, we compare GPTQ’s performance relative to\nstate-of-the-art post-training quantization (PTQ) methods, on ResNet18 and ResNet50, which are\nstandard PTQ benchmarks, in the same setup as (Frantar et al., 2022). As can be seen in Table 1,\nGPTQ performs on par at 4-bit, and slightly worse than the most accurate methods at 3-bit. At the\nsame time, it signiﬁcantly outperforms AdaQuant, the fastest amongst prior PTQ methods. Further,\nwe compare against the full greedy OBQ method on two smaller language models: BERT-base (De-\nvlin et al., 2019) and OPT-125M. The results are shown in Appendix Table 8. At 4 bits, both methods\nperform similarly, and for 3 bits, GPTQ surprisingly performs slightly better. We suspect that this\nis because some of the additional heuristics used by OBQ, such as early outlier rounding, might\nrequire careful adjustments for optimal performance on non-vision models. Overall, GPTQ appears\nto be competitive with state-of-the-art post-training methods for smaller models, while taking only\n< 1 minute rather than ≈1 hour. This enables scaling to much larger models.\nRuntime. Next we measure the full model quantization time (on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU) via\nGPTQ; the results are shown in Table 2. As can be seen, GPTQ quantizes 1-3 billion parameter\nmodels in a matter of minutes and 175B ones in a few hours. For reference, the straight-through\nbased method ZeroQuant-LKD (Yao et al., 2022) reports a 3 hour runtime (on the same hardware)\nfor a 1.3B model, which would linearly extrapolate to several hundred hours (a few weeks) for 175B\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nMethod\nRN18 – 69.76 %\nRN50 – 76.13%\n4bit\n3bit\n4bit\n3bit\nAdaRound\n69.34\n68.37\n75.84\n75.14\nAdaQuant\n68.12\n59.21\n74.68\n64.98\nBRECQ\n69.37\n68.47\n75.88\n75.32\nOBQ\n69.56\n68.69\n75.72\n75.24\nGPTQ\n69.37\n67.88\n75.71\n74.87\nTable 1:\nComparison with state-of-the-art\npost-training methods for vision models.\nOPT\n13B\n30B\n66B\n175B\nRuntime\n20.9m\n44.9m\n1.6h\n4.2h\nBLOOM\n1.7B\n3B\n7.1B\n176B\nRuntime\n2.9m\n5.2m\n10.0m\n3.8h\nTable 2: GPTQ runtime for full quantization\nof the 4 largest OPT and BLOOM models.\nmodels. Adaptive rounding-based methods typically employ a lot more SGD steps and would thus\nbe even more expensive (Nagel et al., 2020; Li et al., 2021).\nLanguage Generation. We begin our large-scale study by compressing the entire OPT and BLOOM\nmodel families to 3- and 4-bit. We then evaluate those models on several language tasks including\nWikiText2 (Merity et al., 2016) (see Figure 1 as well as Tables 3 and 4), Penn Treebank (PTB) (Mar-\ncus et al., 1994) and C4 (Raffel et al., 2020) (both in Appendix A.3). We focus on these perplexity-\nbased tasks, as they are known to be particularly sensitive to model quantization (Yao et al., 2022).\nOn OPT models, GPTQ clearly outperforms RTN, by signiﬁcant margins. For example, GPTQ loses\nonly 0.03 perplexity at 4-bit on the 175B model, while RTN drops 2.2 points, performing worse than\nthe 10× smaller full-precision 13B model. At 3-bit, RTN collapses completely, while GPTQ can still\nmaintain reasonable perplexity, in particular for larger models. BLOOM shows a similar pattern: the\ngaps between methods are however usually a bit smaller, indicating that this model family might be\neasier to quantize. One interesting trend (see also Figure 1) is that larger models generally (with the\nexception of OPT-66B2) appear easier to quantize. This is good news for practical applications, as\nthese are the cases where compression is also the most necessary.\nOPT\nBits\n125M\n350M\n1.3B\n2.7B\n6.7B\n13B\n30B\n66B\n175B\nfull\n16\n27.65\n22.00\n14.63\n12.47\n10.86\n10.13\n9.56\n9.34\n8.34\nRTN\n4\n37.28\n25.94\n48.17\n16.92\n12.10\n11.32\n10.98\n110\n10.54\nGPTQ\n4\n31.12\n24.24\n15.47\n12.87\n11.39\n10.31\n9.63\n9.55\n8.37\nRTN\n3\n1.3e3\n64.57\n1.3e4\n1.6e4\n5.8e3\n3.4e3\n1.6e3\n6.1e3\n7.3e3\nGPTQ\n3\n53.85\n33.79\n20.97\n16.88\n14.86\n11.61\n10.27\n14.16\n8.68\nTable 3: OPT perplexity results on WikiText2.\nBLOOM\nBits\n560M\n1.1B\n1.7B\n3B\n7.1B\n176B\nfull\n16\n22.42\n17.69\n15.39\n13.48\n11.37\n8.11\nRTN\n4\n25.90\n22.00\n16.97\n14.76\n12.10\n8.37\nGPTQ\n4\n24.03\n19.05\n16.48\n14.20\n11.73\n8.21\nRTN\n3\n57.08\n50.19\n63.59\n39.36\n17.38\n571\nGPTQ\n3\n32.31\n25.08\n21.11\n17.40\n13.47\n8.64\nTable 4: BLOOM perplexity results for WikiText2.\n175 Billion Parameter Models. We now examine BLOOM-176B and OPT-175B, the largest dense\nopenly-available models. Table 5 summarizes results across Wikitext-2, PTB, C4. We observe that,\nat 4 bits, GPTQ models reach only ≤0.25 lower perplexity than the full-precision versions, with a\nlarge gap to RTN results on OPT-175B. At 3-bit, RTN collapses, while GPTQ is still able to maintain\ngood performance on most tasks, losing only 0.3 −0.6 points for more than 5× compression. We\nnote that GPTQ’s accuracy can be further improved via ﬁner-granularity grouping (Park et al., 2022):\ngroup-size 1024 (≈0.02 extra bits) improves perplexities by about 0.2 on average and group-size\n128 (≈0.15 extra bits) by another 0.1, which is only 0.1 −0.3 off from the uncompressed accuracy.\n2Upon closer inspection of the OPT-66B model, it appears that this is correlated with the fact that this trained\nmodel has a signiﬁcant fraction of dead units in the early layers, which may make it harder to compress.\n7\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nWe note that grouping interacts very well with GPTQ, as the group parameters can be determined\nduring the quantization process of each layer, always using the most current updated weights.\nMethod\nBits\nOPT-175B\nBLOOM-176B\nWiki2\nPTB\nC4\nLAMB. ↑\nWiki2\nPTB\nC4\nLAMB. ↑\nBaseline\n16\n8.34\n12.01\n10.13\n75.59\n8.11\n14.59\n11.71\n67.40\nRTN\n4\n10.54\n14.22\n11.61\n71.34\n8.37\n15.00\n12.04\n66.70\nGPTQ\n4\n8.37\n12.26\n10.28\n76.80\n8.21\n14.75\n11.81\n67.71\nRTN\n3\n7.3e3\n8.0e3\n4.6e3\n0\n571.\n107.\n598.\n0.17\nGPTQ\n3\n8.68\n12.68\n10.67\n76.19\n8.64\n15.57\n12.27\n65.10\nGPTQ\n3/g1024\n8.45\n12.48\n10.47\n77.39\n8.35\n15.01\n11.98\n67.47\nGPTQ\n3/g128\n8.45\n12.37\n10.36\n76.42\n8.26\n14.89\n11.85\n67.86\nTable 5: Results summary for OPT-175B and BLOOM-176B. “g1024” and “g128” denote results\nwith groupings of size 1024 and 128, respectively.\nPractical Speedups. Finally, we study practical applications. As an interesting use-case, we focus\non the OPT-175B model: quantized to 3 bits, this model takes approximately 63GB of memory,\nincluding the embeddings and the output layer, which are kept in full FP16 precision. Additionally,\nstoring the complete history of keys and values for all layers, a common optimization for generation\ntasks, consumes another ≈9GB for the maximum of 2048 tokens. Hence, we can actually ﬁt\nthe entire quantized model into a single 80GB A100 GPU, which can be executed by dynamically\ndequantizing layers as they are required during inference (the model would not fully ﬁt using 4\nbits). For reference, standard FP16 execution requires 5x80GB GPUs, and the state-of-the-art 8bit\nLLM.int8() quantizer (Dettmers et al., 2022) requires 3 such GPUs.\nNext, we consider language generation, one of the most appealing applications of these models, with\nthe goal of latency reduction. Unlike LLM.int8(), which reduces memory costs but has the same\nruntime as the FP16 baseline, we show that our quantized models can achieve signiﬁcant speedups\nfor this application. For language generation, the model processes and outputs one token at-a-time,\nwhich for OPT-175B can easily take a few 100s of milliseconds per token. Increasing the speed at\nwhich the user receives generated results is challenging, as compute is dominated by matrix-vector\nproducts. Unlike matrix-matrix products, these are primarily limited by memory bandwidth. We\naddress this problem by developing a quantized-matrix full-precision-vector product kernel which\nperforms a matrix vector product by dynamically dequantizing weights when needed. Most notably,\nthis does not require any activation quantization. While dequantization consumes extra compute,\nthe kernel has to access a lot less memory, leading to signiﬁcant speedups, as shown in Table 6. We\nnote that almost all of the speedup is due to our kernels, as communication costs are negligible in\nour standard HuggingFace-accelerate-like setting (see Appendix A.2.2 for details).\nGPU\nFP16\n3bit\nSpeedup\nGPU reduction\nA6000 – 48GB\n589ms\n130ms\n4.53×\n8 →2\nA100 – 80GB\n230ms\n71ms\n3.24×\n5 →1\nTable 6: Average per-token latency (batch size 1) when generating sequences of length 128.\nFor example, using our kernels, the 3-bit OPT-175B model obtained via GPTQ running on a single\nA100 is about 3.25× faster than the FP16 version (running on 5 GPUs) in terms of average time per\ntoken. More accessible GPUs, such as the NVIDIA A6000, have much lower memory bandwidth,\nso this strategy is even more effective: executing the 3-bit OPT-175B model on 2x A6000 GPUs\nreduces latency from 589 milliseconds for FP16 inference (on 8 GPUs) to 130 milliseconds, a 4.5×\nlatency reduction.\nZero-Shot Tasks. While our focus is on language generation, we also evaluate the performance\nof quantized models on some popular zero-shot tasks, namely LAMBADA (Paperno et al., 2016),\nARC (Easy and Challenge) (Boratko et al., 2018) and PIQA (Tata & Patel, 2003). Figure 3 visualizes\nmodel performance on LAMBADA (and see also “Lamb.” results in Table 5). We observe similar\nbehavior as before: the outliers are that 1) quantization appears “easier” across the whole spectrum\nof models at 4-bit, where even RTN performs relatively well, and 2) at 3-bit, RTN breaks down,\nwhile GPTQ still provides good accuracy. We provide additional results in Appendix A.4.\n8\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\n10\n1\n100\n101\n102\n#params in billions\n0.0\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n0.8\nAccuracy on LAMBADA\nOPT Family\n100\n101\n102\n#params in billions\nBLOOM Family\nFP16\n4bit GPTQ\n4bit RTN\n3bit GPTQ\n3bit RTN\nFigure 3: The accuracy of OPT and BLOOM models post-GPTQ, measured on LAMBADA.\nAdditional Tricks. While our experiments so far have focused exclusively on vanilla row-wise\nquantization, we want to emphasize that GPTQ is compatible with essentially any choice of quanti-\nzation grid. For example, it is easily combined with standard grouping (Alistarh et al., 2017; Park\net al., 2022), i.e. applying independent quantization to groups of g consecutive weights. As shown in\nthe last rows of Table 5, this can bring noticeable extra accuracy for the largest models at 3-bit. Fur-\nther, as visualized in Figure 4, it signiﬁcantly reduces the accuracy losses for medium sized models\nat 4-bit precision.\nModel\nFP16\ng128\ng64\ng32\n3-bit\nOPT-175B\n8.34\n9.58\n9.18\n8.94\n8.68\nBLOOM\n8.11\n9.55\n9.17\n8.83\n8.64\nTable 7: 2-bit GPTQ quantization results with\nvarying group-sizes; perplexity on WikiText2.\n100\n101\n#params in billions\n9\n10\n11\n12\n13\n14\n15\n16\nPerplexity on WikiText2\nOPT Models 1.3B to 30B\n4bit\n4bit/g1024\n4bit/g128\nFP16\nFigure 4:\nGPTQ at 4-bit with different\ngroup-sizes on medium sized OPT models.\nExtreme Quantization. Lastly, grouping also makes it possible to achieve reasonable performance\nfor extreme quantization, to around 2-bits per component on average. Table 7 shows results on\nWikiText2 when quantizing the biggest models to 2-bit with varying group-sizes. At ≈2.2 bit\n(group-size 128; using FP16 scale and 2-bit zero point per group) the perplexity increase is already\nless than 1.5 points, while dropping to 0.6 - 0.7 at ≈2.6 bit (group-size 32), which is only slightly\nworse than vanilla 3-bit and might be interesting for practical kernel implementations. Further,\nif we reduce group size to 8, we can apply ternary (-1, 0, +1) quantization, which achieves 9.20\nWikiText2 PPL on OPT-175B, a less than 1 point drop. While this leads to worse compression on\naverage relative to the 2-bit numbers above, this pattern could be efﬁciently implemented on custom\nhardware such as FPGAs. In summary, these results are an encouraging ﬁrst step towards pushing\nhighly-accurate one-shot compression of very large language models, even lower than 3 bits per\nvalue on average.\n6\nSUMMARY AND LIMITATIONS\nWe have presented GPTQ, an approximate second-order method for quantizing truly large language\nmodels. GPTQ can accurately compress some of the largest publicly-available models down to 3\nand 4 bits, which leads to signiﬁcant usability improvements, and to end-to-end speedups, at low\naccuracy loss. We hope that our method will make these models accessible to more researchers and\npractitioners. At the same time, we emphasize some signiﬁcant limitations: On the technical side,\nour method obtains speedups from reduced memory movement, and does not lead to computational\nreductions. In addition, our study focuses on generative tasks, and does not consider activation\nquantization. These are natural directions for future work, and we believe this can be achieved with\ncarefully-designed GPU kernels and existing techniques (Yao et al., 2022; Wu et al., 2022).\n9\n\n\n--- PAGE 10 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nACKNOWLEDGMENTS\nElias Frantar and Dan Alistarh gratefully acknowledge funding from the European Research Coun-\ncil (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 programme (grant agreement No. 805223\nScaleML), as well as experimental support from Eldar Kurtic, and from the IST Austria IT de-\npartment, in particular Stefano Elefante, Andrei Hornoiu, and Alois Schloegl. The work of Saleh\nAshkboos and Torsten Hoeﬂer was supported by the PASC DaCeMI project, received EuroHPC-JU\nfunding under grant MAELSTROM, No. 955513. We thank the Swiss National Supercomputing\nCenter (CSCS) for supporting us with compute infrastructure.\n7\nETHICS STATEMENT\nOur work introduces a general method for compressing large language models (LLMs) via quan-\ntization, with little-to-no accuracy loss in terms of standard accuracy metrics such as perplexity.\nOur method is task-agnostic, as it only uses a tiny amount of randomly-chosen data for calibration.\nWe therefore do not foresee any signiﬁcant ethical implications arising directly from the technical\ndetails of our method. However, one possible consideration is that our study focused on “leading\naccuracy” metrics that are standard in the literature, such as perplexity, which is essentially standard\nin the literature (Dettmers et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022). We believe a thorough study of the impact\nof compression upon secondary measures, and in particular bias effects (Bender et al., 2021) is war-\nranted, and may be rendered easier through our work. At the same time, our work makes inference\non extremely large language models more accessible, for better or for worse. We believe that, in\ntime, such tools will become much easier to use and deploy, making the need to understand their\npower and limitations even more stringent.\n8\nREPRODUCIBILITY STATEMENT\nIn the Supplementary Materials, we provide code to reproduce all experiments in this paper. More\nspeciﬁcally, this includes:\n• Compressing all models from the OPT and BLOOM model families to 2/3/4 bits.\n• Evaluating perplexity of the quantized models.\n• Our 3-bit CUDA kernel together with compressed inference benchmarking features.\n• Code for the ZeroShot experiments.\n• A README ﬁle providing sample commands and information on how to run all scripts.\nREFERENCES\nDan Alistarh, Demjan Grubic, Jerry Li, Ryota Tomioka, and Milan Vojnovic. (2016) and OPT-125M/WikiText2, which is one of the largest models to which OBQ\ncan be reasonably applied.\nMethod\nBERT-base\nOPT-125M\n88.53 F1 ↑\n27.66 PPL ↓\n4bit\n3bit\n4bit\n3bit\nOBQ\n88.23\n85.29\n32.52\n69.32\nGPTQ\n88.18\n86.02\n31.12\n53.85\nTable 8: Comparison of GPTQ relative to OBQ on BERT-base/SQuAD and OPT-125M/WikiText2.\nA.2\nEXPERIMENT DETAILS\nThis section provides additional details about our experiment setup, in particular regarding the model\nevaluation and the setup of our timing experiments.\nA.2.1\nEVALUATION\nFor language generation experiments, we calculate the perplexity, in standard fashion like Radford\net al. (2019), as follows: First, the entire validation set is concatenated using two linebreaks as\nseparators and encoded using the default HuggingFace tokenizer of each model. Next, the sequence\nis split into non-overlapping segments of width 2048, the full context size of our models. These are\nsent through the model to collect the log-probabilities corresponding to the next token each. Their\nexponentiated average is the ﬁnal perplexity we report.\nFor zero-shot tasks we follow the EleutherAI evaluation harness3 in terms of data preprocessing and\nﬁnal score calculation. We note that we evaluate all individual samples separately and thus do not\napply any padding.\nA.2.2\nTIMING EXPERIMENT SETUP\nOur timing experiments are performed following the standard HuggingFace/accelerate4 setup also\nused by the recent work LLM.int8() (Dettmers et al., 2022). In this setting, the model is split by\ndistributing chunks of consecutive layers across GPUs. Importantly, in this setup the communication\ncosts are minimal, < 5% of the total runtime even when working with 8 GPUs. This means almost\nall of the reported speedups are due to our quantized-matrix full-precision vector product kernels.\nWe emphasize that the only difference between the FP16 baseline and our quantized models are the\nkernels used to perform the underlying matrix-vector products.\nThis means all overheads due to HuggingFace, attention or non-quantized operations like residuals\nor LayerNorms are exactly the same. Consequently, our quantized models should beneﬁt from more\nadvanced distribution strategies (Zheng et al., 2022) or more efﬁcient attention kernels (Dao et al.,\n2022) just as much as our baseline.\nIn general, our kernels target generative inference in the low batch-size setting (for simplicity, we\nconsider only batchsize 1) where the underlying (close to) matrix-vector products are memory-\nbound. For non-generative and large-batch applications, operations may be compute- rather than\nmemory-bound and our kernels thus not directly applicable. Instead, one could simply decompress\nthe matrix before performing the corresponding matrix-matrix calculations: this takes < 1.5ms on\nan A100 and < 3ms on an A6000 compared to 76ms/365ms for the subsequent OPT-175B FC2 layer\ncomputation with batchsize 16×1024 tokens. Hence, for such applications our methods signiﬁcantly\nreduce the required number of GPUs at very little computational overhead. This is similar to recent\nwork (Dettmers et al., 2022), but we achieve a 2.5× higher compression rate.\n3https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness\n4https://huggingface.co/docs/accelerate/index\n13\n\n\n--- PAGE 14 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2023\nA.3\nADDITIONAL LANGUAGE GENERATION RESULTS\nTables 9, 10, 11 and 12 show additional results for language generation tasks.\nOPT\nBits\n125M\n350M\n1.3B\n2.7B\n6.7B\n13B\n30B\n66B\n175B\nfull\n16\n38.99\n31.08\n20.29\n17.97\n15.77\n14.52\n14.04\n13.36\n12.01\nRTN\n4\n53.89\n36.79\n57.30\n31.05\n18.84\n16.51\n15.40\n225.66\n14.22\nGPTQ\n4\n45.17\n34.52\n21.85\n19.14\n16.56\n14.94\n14.26\n13.81\n12.26\nRTN\n3\n1.4e3\n88.04\n1.3e4\n1.4e4\n5.7e3\n2.8e3\n1.2e3\n5.0e3\n8.0e3\nGPTQ\n3\n73.19\n47.08\n32.10\n24.81\n21.88\n16.68\n15.36\n28.12\n12.86\nTable 9: OPT perplexity results on PTB.\nBLOOM\nBits\n560M\n1.1B\n1.7B\n3B\n7.1B\n176B\nfull\n16\n43.69\n57.96\n30.00\n25.34\n20.83\n14.59\nRTN\n4\n51.10\n66.85\n33.58\n27.68\n22.42\n15.00\nGPTQ\n4\n46.97\n62.47\n31.84\n26.49\n21.67\n14.75\nRTN\n3\n126.\n185.\n106.\n66.78\n35.04\n107.\nGPTQ\n3\n70.35\n87.04\n46.11\n34.02\n26.14\n15.57\nTable 10: BLOOM perplexity results for PTB.\nOPT\nBits\n125M\n350M\n1.3B\n2.7B\n6.7B\n13B\n30B\n66B\n175B\nfull\n16\n26.56\n22.59\n16.07\n14.34\n12.71\n12.06\n11.44\n10.99\n10.13\nRTN\n4\n33.91\n26.21\n24.51\n18.43\n14.36\n13.36\n13.46\n309.\n11.61\nGPTQ\n4\n29.22\n24.63\n16.97\n15.00\n13.18\n12.26\n11.57\n11.23\n10.28\nRTN\n3\n834\n55.49\n5.2e3\n1.1e4\n5.3e3\n3.1e3\n1.4e3\n3.5e3\n4.6e3\nGPTQ\n3\n42.41\n31.33\n21.63\n18.17\n17.14\n13.34\n12.23\n14.59\n10.67\nTable 11: OPT perplexity results on C4. "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 41497], [44000, 56000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 56815, "selected_characters": 53497, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-TORCH-NUMERIC/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "bb6b2d8154a10f8f3b15e5ae4465cbc8d833067046964fc6b4d66dc036131b45", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/source/notes/numerical_accuracy.rst =====\n.. _numerical_accuracy:\n\nNumerical accuracy\n==================\n\nIn modern computers, floating point numbers are represented using IEEE 754 standard.\nFor more details on floating point arithmetic and IEEE 754 standard, please see\n`Floating point arithmetic <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating-point_arithmetic>`_\nIn particular, note that floating point provides limited accuracy (about 7 decimal digits\nfor single precision floating point numbers, about 16 decimal digits for double precision\nfloating point numbers) and that floating point addition and multiplication are not\nassociative, so the order of the operations affects the results.\nBecause of this, PyTorch is not guaranteed\nto produce bitwise identical results for floating point computations that are\nmathematically identical. Similarly, bitwise identical results are not guaranteed across\nPyTorch releases, individual commits, or different platforms. In particular, CPU and GPU\nresults can be different even for bitwise-identical inputs and even after controlling for\nthe sources of randomness.\n\nBatched computations or slice computations\n------------------------------------------\n\nMany operations in PyTorch support batched computation, where the same operation is performed\nfor the elements of the batches of inputs. An example of this is :meth:`torch.mm` and\n:meth:`torch.bmm`. It is possible to implement batched computation as a loop over batch elements,\nand apply the necessary math operations to the individual batch elements, for efficiency reasons\nwe are not doing that, and typically perform computation for the whole batch. The mathematical\nlibraries that we are calling, and PyTorch internal implementations of operations can produce\nslightly different results in this case, compared to non-batched computations. In particular,\nlet ``A`` and ``B`` be 3D tensors with the dimensions suitable for batched matrix multiplication.\nThen ``(A@B)[0]`` (the first element of the batched result) is not guaranteed to be bitwise\nidentical to ``A[0]@B[0]`` (the matrix product of the first elements of the input batches)\neven though mathematically it's an identical computation.\n\nSimilarly, an operation applied to a tensor slice is not guaranteed to produce results that are\nidentical to the slice of the result of the same operation applied to the full tensor. E.g. let\n``A`` be a 2-dimensional tensor. ``A.sum(-1)[0]`` is not guaranteed to be bitwise equal to\n``A[:,0].sum()``.\n\n\nExtremal values\n---------------\n\nWhen inputs contain large values such that intermediate results may overflow the range of the\nused datatype, the end result may overflow too, even though it is representable in the original\ndatatype. E.g.:\n\n.. code:: python\n\n    import torch\n    a=torch.tensor([1e20, 1e20]) # fp32 type by default\n    a.norm() # produces tensor(inf)\n    a.double().norm() # produces tensor(1.4142e+20, dtype=torch.float64), representable in fp32\n\n.. _Linear Algebra Stability:\n\nLinear algebra (``torch.linalg``)\n---------------------------------\n\nNon-finite values\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nThe external libraries (backends) that ``torch.linalg`` uses provide no guarantees on their behaviour\nwhen the inputs have non-finite values like ``inf`` or ``NaN``. As such, neither does PyTorch.\nThe operations may return a tensor with non-finite values, or raise an exception, or even segfault.\n\nConsider using :func:`torch.isfinite` before calling these functions to detect this situation.\n\nExtremal values in linalg\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nFunctions within ``torch.linalg`` have more `Extremal Values`_ than other PyTorch functions.\n\n:ref:`linalg solvers` and :ref:`linalg inverses` assume that the input matrix ``A`` is invertible. If it is close to\nbeing non-invertible (for example, if it has a very small singular value), then these algorithms may silently return\nincorrect results. These matrices are said to be `ill-conditioned <https://nhigham.com/2020/03/19/what-is-a-condition-number/>`_.\nIf provided with ill-conditioned inputs, the result of these functions they may vary when using the same inputs on different\ndevices or when using different backends via the keyword ``driver``.\n\nSpectral operations like ``svd``, ``eig``, and ``eigh`` may also return incorrect results (and their gradients may be infinite)\nwhen their inputs have singular values that are close to each other. This is because the algorithms used to compute these decompositions\nstruggle to converge for these inputs.\n\nRunning the computation in ``float64`` (as NumPy does by default) often helps, but it does not solve these issues in all cases.\nAnalyzing the spectrum of the inputs via :func:`torch.linalg.svdvals` or their condition number via :func:`torch.linalg.cond`\nmay help to detect these issues.\n\n\nTensorFloat-32(TF32) on Nvidia Ampere (and later) devices\n---------------------------------------------------------\n\nOn Ampere (and later) Nvidia GPUs, PyTorch can use TensorFloat32 (TF32) to speed up mathematically intensive operations, in particular matrix multiplications and convolutions.\nWhen an operation is performed using TF32 tensor cores, only the first 10 bits of the input mantissa are read.\nThis may reduce accuracy and produce surprising results (e.g., multiplying a matrix by the identity matrix may produce results that are different from the input).\nBy default, TF32 tensor cores are disabled for matrix multiplications and enabled for convolutions, although most neural network workloads have the same convergence behavior when using TF32 as they have with fp32.\nWe recommend enabling TF32 tensor cores for matrix multiplications with ``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.fp32_precision = \"tf32\"`` (```torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_tf32 = True`` is going to be deprecated) if your network does not need full float32 precision.\nIf your network needs full float32 precision for both matrix multiplications and convolutions, then TF32 tensor cores can also be disabled for convolutions with ``torch.backends.cudnn.conv.fp32_precision = \"ieee\"`` (``torch.backends.cudnn.allow_tf32 = False`` is going to be deprecated).\n\nFor more information see :ref:`TensorFloat32<tf32_on_ampere>`.\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 GEMMs\n----------------------------------------------------\nHalf-precision GEMM operations are typically done with intermediate accumulations (reduction) in single-precision for numerical accuracy and improved resilience to overflow. For performance, certain GPU architectures, especially more recent ones, allow a few truncations of the intermediate accumulation results to the reduced precision (e.g., half-precision). This change is often benign from the perspective of model convergence, though it may lead to unexpected results (e.g., ``inf`` values when the final result should be be representable in half-precision).\nIf reduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nA similar flag exists for BF16 GEMM operations and is turned on by default. If BF16\nreduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nFor more information see :ref:`allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction<fp16reducedprecision>` and :ref:`allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction<bf16reducedprecision>`\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 in Scaled Dot Product Attention (SDPA)\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nA naive SDPA math backend, when using FP16/BF16 inputs, can accumulate significant numerical errors due to the usage of low-precision intermediate buffers. To mitigate this issue, the default behavior now involves upcasting FP16/BF16 inputs to FP32. Computations are performed in FP32/TF32, and the final FP32 results are then downcasted back to FP16/BF16. This will improve numerical accuracy of the final output for the math backend with FP16/BF16 inputs, but increases memory usages and may cause the performance regressions in the math backend as computations shift from FP16/BF16 BMM to FP32/TF32 BMM/Matmul.\n\nFor scenarios where reduced-precision reductions are preferred for speed, they can be enabled with the following setting:\n``torch.backends.cuda.allow_fp16_bf16_reduction_math_sdp(True)``\n\n.. _fp16_on_mi200:\n\nReduced Precision FP16 and BF16 GEMMs and Convolutions on AMD Instinct MI200 devices\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nOn AMD Instinct MI200 GPUs, the FP16 and BF16 V_DOT2 and MFMA matrix instructions flush input and output denormal values to zero. FP32 and FP64 MFMA matrix instructions do not flush input and output denormal values to zero. The affected instructions are only used by rocBLAS (GEMM) and MIOpen (convolution) kernels; all other PyTorch operations will not encounter this behavior. All other supported AMD GPUs will not encounter this behavior.\n\nrocBLAS and MIOpen provide alternate implementations for affected FP16 operations. Alternate implementations for BF16 operations are not provided; BF16 numbers have a larger dynamic range than FP16 numbers and are less likely to encounter denormal values. For the FP16 alternate implementations, FP16 input values are cast to an intermediate BF16 value and then cast back to FP16 output after the accumulate FP32 operations. In this way, the input and output types are unchanged.\n\nWhen training using FP16 precision, some models may fail to converge with FP16 denorms flushed to zero. Denormal values more frequently occur in the backward pass of training during gradient calculation. PyTorch by default will use the rocBLAS and MIOpen alternate implementations during the backward pass. The default behavior can be overridden using environment variables, ROCBLAS_INTERNAL_FP16_ALT_IMPL and MIOPEN_DEBUG_CONVOLUTION_ATTRIB_FP16_ALT_IMPL. The behavior of these environment variables is as follows:\n\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n|               | forward   | backward  |\n+===============+===========+===========+\n| Env unset     | original  | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 1  | alternate | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 0  | original  | original  |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n\nThe following is the list of operations where rocBLAS may be used:\n\n* torch.addbmm\n* torch.addmm\n* torch.baddbmm\n* torch.bmm\n* torch.mm\n* torch.nn.GRUCell\n* torch.nn.LSTMCell\n* torch.nn.Linear\n* torch.sparse.addmm\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend implementations:\n\n  * slowNd\n  * slowNd_transposed\n  * slowNd_dilated\n  * slowNd_dilated_transposed\n\nThe following is the list of operations where MIOpen may be used:\n\n* torch.nn.Conv[Transpose]Nd\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend
